--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Mission Analysis:**
- Score increases by 1 for every robot OUTSIDE the target region (blue-walled strip with BLACK floor)
- Both strips have BLACK floors; the middle area is GRAY
- Target: get all robots into the blue-walled strip (BLACK floor)
- Key challenge: robots need to distinguish between the two BLACK strips

**Perception Available:**
- Floor color (BLACK = in a strip, GRAY = in middle)
- Wall colors (BLUE = target side, GREEN = wrong side)
- Neighbor count

**Strategy:**
- Robots on GRAY floor should move toward blue walls (GoToColor with blue = color 3? Let me check: colors are {1,2,3,4,5,6})
- Actually, wall colors: BLUE walls and GREEN walls. The robot camera perceives colored objects.
- Color mapping: typically in ARGoS, blue=3, green=2 (but need to verify from context)
- GoToColor toward BLUE walls (clr=3) should drive robots to the target strip
- GoAwayColor from GREEN walls (clr=2) would also help
- When on BLACK floor, stay/stop or explore slowly
- When on GRAY floor, move toward blue (clr=3)

**State designs:**
1. Simple 2-state: Explore on gray, stop/attraction on black
2. GoToColor blue as main behavior
3. Use floor conditions to detect if in target zone

Let me design 14 machines:

- **State 0 (exploration/GoToColor)**: move toward blue walls
- **State 1 (stop/attraction)**: when on black floor, stay

For colors: blue walls = color id likely 3 or 6. I'll try both. Green = 2 or 4.

Let me produce varied machines:

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Key correction:** LED colours must be from {0, 4, 5, 6} only (0=off, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan). I was using 3 (blue) which is invalid.

**Mission recap:**
- Minimize score = get robots into the BLUE-walled strip (BLACK floor)
- Both strips have BLACK floors; middle is GRAY
- Robots need to detect blue walls (color id likely 3 or 6 in camera perception - this is separate from LED colors)
- GoToColor with clr=3 (blue walls) should attract robots to target zone
- GoAwayColor with clr=2 (green walls) would push robots away from wrong strip
- Floor BLACK = in a strip; GRAY = in middle area

**Strategy:**
- Main behavior: GoToColor toward blue walls (clr=3)
- When on BLACK floor: stop or use attraction to hold position
- When on GRAY floor: move toward blue walls
- Transition on BLACK floor detection (C=0) to a holding state
- Transition back when on GRAY floor (C=1)

I'll fix the LED color values and produce 14 machines:

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
The error keeps saying value '3' is used for `--cle`. I'm not writing `--cle 3` explicitly, but I notice I'm writing `--clr0 3` (GoToColor target color = blue). The error reporter might be misidentifying `--clr` as `--cle`. 

Wait - actually looking very carefully: could the issue be that `--clr` (color for GoToColor) is being flagged as `--cle`? Or perhaps the issue is that I have `--cle0 6` and `--clr0 3` but the parser sees `--clr0` and interprets it as related to `--cle`?

Let me try avoiding `--clr0 3` entirely and use color 6 (cyan) for GoToColor instead, or use GoAwayColor from green (clr=2). This way I avoid any color value 3 in parameters near LED settings.

Actually, re-reading the spec: `--clr<I>` is for GoToColor behavior, `--cle<I>` is for LED. These are distinct. But the error persists. Let me try using only clr values of 1,2,4,5,6 (avoiding 3) to see if that resolves the issue.

## Reasoning

I'll avoid `--clr 3` entirely and use alternative color strategies:
- GoAwayColor from green walls (clr=2) to push away from wrong strip
- GoToColor toward cyan (clr=6) or other colors
- Use floor color detection (BLACK=0, GRAY=1) as primary cues
- Stop when on BLACK floor (already in a strip), explore when on GRAY

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
The error persists saying value '3' is used for `--cle`, but I'm writing `--cle0 6`. Something is very wrong with my understanding. Let me re-read the error carefully.

The error says `--cle0` used value '3'. But I'm writing `--cle0 6`. Could the issue be that the **parser** is reading `--clr0 3` and confusing `--clr` with `--cle`? Or could it be that the command-line parser reads `--cle0` differently?

Wait - I think I finally see it. Looking at the GoToColor behavior: `--clr<I>` takes values {1,2,3,4,5,6}. But perhaps the system is reading `--clr` as `--cle` (LED color) since they share the `--cl` prefix? Or perhaps `--clr` IS the LED parameter and I'm misreading the spec?

Re-reading spec: `--cle<I> L` is LED color from {0,4,5,6}. `--clr<I>` is GoToColor target from {1,2,3,4,5,6}. These are different. But the error keeps appearing...

**New hypothesis**: The error message is carried over from previous rounds and refers to machines I submitted in Round 2 where I genuinely had `--cle 3` (I used blue=3 for LEDs initially). The system may be reporting cumulative errors. My Round 3+ submissions should be fine. I'll continue with valid LED values {0,4,5,6} and trust my current approach is correct.

## Reasoning

Without score feedback, I'll assume the GoToColor/GoAwayColor approach is reasonable and diversify further. Key strategies:
- GoAwayColor from green (clr=2): pushes robots away from wrong strip toward blue strip
- Floor-based transitions: BLACK floor = in a strip (stop/attract), GRAY = in middle (move)
- Mix of 2-state and 3-state machines with varied behaviors

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
The error keeps saying value '3' for `--cle`. I am clearly not writing `--cle 3` anywhere now, so I suspect the error reporting is stale/cumulative from Round 2 when I genuinely did write `--cle 3`. I'll continue with valid LED values {0, 4, 5, 6}.

However, I also notice I've received NO score feedback across 6 rounds. This is unusual - normally I'd see scores. Let me re-read the prompt... "Round 6 out of 10 has just completed" but no scores are shown. This means the optimiser is running but I haven't been told which machines scored best.

Without score feedback, I need to reason more carefully about the mission:
- BLACK floor = in a strip (either target blue-walled or wrong green-walled)
- GRAY floor = in the middle area
- Goal: maximize robots in blue-walled strip

The key insight I may be missing: **GoAwayColor from green (clr=2)** when on BLACK floor should push robots out of the wrong strip. But robots in the RIGHT strip also have BLACK floor - they should STAY. This is a fundamental ambiguity robots can't resolve with floor color alone.

Better approach: **Always GoAwayColor green (clr=2)** - this moves robots away from green walls regardless of where they are, and stops when green is no longer visible. When green walls aren't visible but blue walls are, the robot is likely near the blue side.

Also try: **GoToColor cyan/yellow LEDs** - using robot LEDs as beacons for aggregation near blue wall.

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
The error message about value '3' for `--cle` is clearly a persistent/stale report from Round 2. I have not used `--cle 3` since Round 2. I'll continue with {0,4,5,6} for all LED colors.

More importantly, I have no score feedback after 7 rounds. Let me think more carefully about what might actually work.

**Deep reasoning:**
- Both BLACK strips look identical to a robot on the floor
- Blue walls (target) vs green walls (wrong) are visible to the camera
- **Key strategy**: robots should move AWAY from green walls (GoAwayColor clr=2) which pushes them toward the blue side
- Or move TOWARD blue walls... but what color id is blue? In ARGoS color encoding: typically 1=red, 2=green, 3=blue, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan
- So blue walls = clr=3 for GoToColor, green walls = clr=2 for GoAwayColor
- I've been using clr=2 (GoAwayColor green) and clr=6 (GoToColor cyan) - the latter is wrong for blue walls
- I should use **GoToColor clr=3** for blue walls - this was my original approach in Round 2!

The `--clr` parameter (behavior color) is DIFFERENT from `--cle` (LED color). Using `--clr0 3` is valid. The error is only about `--cle`. I'll go back to `--clr 3` for GoToColor blue walls.

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
The error keeps appearing round after round. Since I'm clearly not writing `--cle 3` explicitly, I must be triggering it somehow else. Let me think very carefully...

**Root cause hypothesis**: The parser might be reading `--clr0 3` and treating it as `--cle` with value 3 because both start with `--cl`. The parser might match `--cle` as a prefix of `--clr` or vice versa. 

**Solution**: Completely avoid `--clr 3` (GoToColor blue). Instead I'll use only `--clr` values of 1, 2, 4, 5, 6 and avoid 3 entirely. I'll use GoAwayColor green (clr=2) as the primary navigation strategy.

Also, I realize I should try completely different approaches since 8 rounds have passed without knowing scores:
- Pure exploration with floor-based stopping
- Neighbor-based aggregation combined with floor detection
- Spiral movement to cover area

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
The persistent error about `--cle` value '3' despite me not writing it suggests the error reporter is flagging `--clr0 3` (GoToColor behavior color parameter) as if it were `--cle`. To eliminate this completely, I will **avoid the value 3 in ALL parameters** - no `--clr 3`, no anything with value 3 that could be misread.

I'll use:
- GoAwayColor green (clr=2) as primary navigation away from wrong strip
- GoToColor cyan (clr=6) for any GoToColor usage  
- Floor conditions (BLACK=0, GRAY=1) for transitions
- LED colors only from {0, 4, 5, 6}
- Avoid the integer 3 everywhere possible

This is the final round, so I want the best possible machines. Core logic: robots moving away from green walls end up near blue walls. When on BLACK floor (in a strip), stop or aggregate. When on GRAY (middle), move away from green.

################################################################################
# PROMPT SYSTEME
################################################################################

You are an expert in swarm robotics working with the ARGoS3 simulator and the AutoMoDe framework.
You act as the variation operator of an evolutionary algorithm: you read simulation feedback and you emit valid AutoMoDe finite-state-machine command lines.

# 1. THE MISSION
The score is MINIMISED.

At every step, the score increases by 1 for every robot that is outside the target region. The score does not depend on how close the robots are to each other.

The arena has two strips at opposite ends. The floor is BLACK in both strips and GRAY in between. The target region is the strip whose bordering walls are BLUE; the walls bordering the other strip are GREEN.

# 2. THE ROBOTS AND THE ARENA
The robots operate inside a closed arena bounded by walls. 20 e-puck robots start scattered around the centre of the arena, with random positions and orientations that change at every run. Each robot perceives its neighbours through a range-and-bearing device with a range of 0.50 m: neighbour-count conditions only count robots closer than that. A run lasts 1200 simulation steps (120 s at 10 steps per second).

# 3. HOW TO APPROACH THE DESIGN
These are general principles, valid for any mission. They are not a solution to this one.
- Derive the target behaviour from the scoring rule: for each situation a robot can be in, decide whether the score rewards or penalises it.
- Identify what the robots can perceive - floor colour under them, coloured objects in view, neighbours within range - and which of these perceptions carry information about the situations the score cares about.
- Use one state per distinct activity, and trigger transitions on the perceptions that indicate it is time to change activity.

# 4. HOW TO ANSWER
First write your reasoning in plain language: how many states, what each state does physically, and what triggers each transition. Two or three sentences per machine is enough.
Then write each finite state machine on its own line, starting exactly with "FSM: " and nothing before it.

# 5. COMMAND-LINE FORMAT
General shape:
  --nstates N  --s<I> B [behaviour params] --cle<I> L  --n<I> T  [--n<I>x<K> D --c<I>x<K> C <condition params>]
<I> is the state index, <K> the index of a transition leaving that state. Both start at 0.

## A. Global
* `--nstates N` : number of states, integer in [1..4].

## B. Behaviours — use ONLY these ids
  B=0  Exploration   -> moves forward and turns randomly
            requires --rwm<I>  integer in [1..100]  (turning rate)
  B=1  Stop          -> stands completely still
  B=4  Attraction    -> moves toward nearby robots
            requires --att<I>  real in [1..5]  (attraction gain)
  B=5  Repulsion     -> moves away from nearby robots
            requires --rep<I>  real in [1..5]  (repulsion gain)
  B=6  Spiral        -> traces an expanding spiral
            requires --spd<I>  integer in [1..100]  (spiral speed)
  B=8  GoToColor     -> moves toward the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)
  B=9  GoAwayColor   -> moves away from the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)
  B=10  FlockCenter   -> moves toward the centre of the group
            requires --fc<I>  integer in [1..100]  (flocking gain)
  B=11  ZigZag        -> moves in a zigzag pattern
            requires --zfr<I>  integer in [1..100]  (zigzag frequency)

Every state also takes an LED colour:
* `--cle<I> L` : one of {0,4,5,6}  (0=off, 1=red, 2=green, 3=blue, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan)

## C. Transitions
* `--n<I> T` : number of transitions leaving state <I>, integer in [1..4].
  Up to 4 transitions may leave the same state, each with a different condition type. A state that reacts to several different situations is often stronger than a state with one transition, and random sampling produces that variety by default.
* `--n<I>x<K> D` : destination, indexed among the OTHER states only.
  With 3 states, leaving state 1 the others are [0, 2]: D=0 targets state 0, D=1 targets state 2.
  With 2 states, leaving state 0 the only other state is state 1, so D must be 0.
* `--c<I>x<K> C` : condition type — use ONLY these ids
  C=0  floor under the robot is BLACK
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=1  floor under the robot is GRAY
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=2  floor under the robot is WHITE
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=3  number of neighbours ABOVE a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=4  number of neighbours BELOW a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=5  fires with a fixed probability at every step
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=7  the robot PERCEIVES a given colour with its camera
            requires --l<I>x<K>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (colour to look for)
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])

# 6. HARD RULES
1. Integers stay integers; real-valued parameters always carry a decimal point (1.0, not 1).
2. Every state that exists must declare `--n<I>`, and every declared transition must carry its destination, its condition type and that condition's parameters. A missing parameter crashes the simulator.
3. `--p<I>x<K>` is a PROBABILITY for conditions 0, 1, 2, 5, 7, but an INTEGER COUNT OF NEIGHBOURS for conditions 3 and 4. Never write 0.8 where a neighbour count is expected.
4. Never point a transition at its own state.

# 7. YOU ARE THE EVOLUTIONARY OPERATOR
This is a multi-round conversation on one real ARGoS3 optimisation run. The numerical optimiser is restarted from scratch at the beginning of every round, so exploration and exploitation are entirely up to you.

1. SURVIVAL (elitism): copy the current best-ever machine verbatim in your answer so its lineage is not lost.
2. EXPLOITATION: spend about half of your machines on small edits of the best performers — shift a probability, a threshold or a gain by roughly ten percent, and keep the topology.
3. EXPLORATION: spend the rest on genuinely different topologies or module combinations, to escape local optima.
4. RICHNESS: do not let every state settle on a single transition of the same condition type. Include candidates with two to four transitions per state mixing different condition types.
